Both land `partial`: #260 parked on `customers` (a survey of testers alone answers a question about testers), #261 on `live_listings` (five of seven stages are facts about a journey somebody walked). #261's listing half runs today and is the half that stays true for every buyer who has not arrived yet. | Yes |
| B-413 | 2026-09-20 | A forecast that was not written down before its period is refused, not calibrated against. | Recalibration from remembered expectations is the most reliable way to build a model that is always well calibrated and never right: the expectation adjusts to the outcome on the way past, sincerely, and nobody involved notices it happening. `Forecast.made_on` must precede `period.starts_on`, and a forecast dated during or after its own period raises. It is the guard the rest of #262 rests on and the one that is inconvenient exactly when it matters. | Yes |
| B-414 | 2026-09-20 | The forecast error is decomposed in log space, never by a waterfall. | Revenue is a product of seven terms, and substituting them one at a time gives each term a different share depending where in the sequence it is walked -- so the person doing the decomposition chooses, after seeing the numbers, how much of the miss belongs to the term they would rather blame. In logs the split is exact and order-independent, with no interaction term and no sequence to pick. What logs cannot do is invent a reading, so a term with no measured actual lands in `unexplained` rather than being distributed across the measured ones: the same rule as B-370's cost attribution, and for the same reason -- the unmodelled term is always the one nobody has a story for. A decomposition whose terms do not multiply back to the revenue within 0.5% is refused outright, because splitting that error would attribute a bookkeeping disagreement to conversion. | Yes |
| B-415 | 2026-09-20 | Optimistic model error is penalised five times as hard as pessimistic error, automatically, and recovers on a ratchet. | Under-forecasting is a pleasant surprise. Over-forecasting is a plan built on money that did not arrive, and by the time the miss is visible the spending it justified has been committed. The penalty applies without anybody agreeing to it -- there is no argument `ceiling()` accepts that raises it -- and recovery needs three consecutive accurate periods before it begins, then moves one step at a time. Symmetric recovery would let a single good month erase a year of being wrong in the same direction, which is how a persistently optimistic model reports itself as calibrated. | Yes |
| B-416 | 2026-09-20 | Calibration produces a ceiling on confidence, never a probability of its own. | `scale.confidence` owns the CA$5K probability and is built to be incapable of being argued upward. A second number answering the same question means the more flattering one gets quoted (B-379). So #262 returns a cap that `probability()` applies in one direction only, alongside the weakest-rung minimum and the #275 evidence gate. | Yes |
| B-417 | 2026-09-20 | A period with no live listings is excluded from calibration, not scored as a forecast miss. | Every period today is that period: nothing is published, so a calibration run over them would report a catastrophically optimistic model and take confidence to the floor -- arithmetic about an unopened shop rather than evidence about a model, and the same defect as B-372's marginal value from zero attributed orders. Excluded periods are counted and named in the result, so the exclusion is visible rather than a quietly smaller denominator. | Yes |
| B-418 | 2026-09-20 | Engineering capacity and marketing capacity run on different clocks, and the module exists to keep them apart. | Engineering leaves an occasion when its last lane closes -- nothing new can be launched, indexed and finished in time. Marketing leaves weeks later, at the buyer's own last practical make date, because the catalogue already listed goes on selling until then. Those weeks earn most: a maker buying a quick gift pattern on the 10th of December is the most motivated buyer of the year, and a shop budgeting one capacity against one calendar has already moved its attention to February. `seasonal.compression` had the insight in its mode names; nothing held the two as separate ledgers. The rule this states is that engineering rolls forward first and marketing rolls forward last. | Yes |
| B-419 | 2026-09-20 | Rolling capacity forward is refused in both directions, early and late. | Leaving an occasion that still holds a capacity abandons the window that earns most. Arriving at one whose own preparation lead has already passed is the same mistake with a later date on it: capacity moved out of a season that is ending into one that is already late has not been rescued, it has been spent on being late twice. `PREPARATION_LEADS` already prices what a receiving occasion needs, so both checks are arithmetic rather than judgement. The plan returns its refusals alongside its moves, because a plan listing only moves reads identically whether nothing needed moving or everything was blocked. | Yes |
| B-420 | 2026-09-20 | The demand floor is computed; the decay before it is reported as unobserved. | "Roll capacity forward before demand collapses" implies a curve, and only its hard floor is derivable: past the buyer's last practical make date demand is zero because nobody can finish the object. Real demand falls earlier -- makers do not all wait for the last possible day -- but no listing of this shop's has ever been watched through a season, and a decay date taken from a plausible shape is a point estimate presented as a fact, which is the move `seasonal.uncertainty` exists to refuse. `demand_curve()` names what would settle it: one occasion's daily impressions and orders for a listing that was live through it. | Yes |
| B-421 | 2026-09-20 | A new module's basename is checked against the whole repository, not just its own package. | The seasonal capacity work was first written as `seasonal/arbitrage.py`, and `radar/arbitrage.py` already existed; the second candidate name, `capacity`, was taken by `intel/capacity.py`. Neither would have collided on import, so nothing would have broken -- it would only have meant two unrelated modules answering to one name in a codebase where imports are read more often than written. Renamed to `seasonal/rollforward.py`, after the requirement's own verb. B-364 was recorded after a `cat >` destroyed a file whose name was already in use; this is the same check applied one step earlier, when it costs a rename instead of a recovery. | Yes |
| B-422 | 2026-09-20 | A meta-agent's score is realised uplift, and activity is arithmetically unable to reach it. | #179's own last clause -- measured by verified uplift, not number of changes -- is implemented as a function that cannot count proposals rather than as a rule saying it should not. A swarm graded on changes made will make changes: forty findings, thirty proposals, twelve promotions, and no capability curve anywhere that moved, with every number on the way pointing upward. The other half matters as much: a rejected proposal scores zero and never negative. Penalising rejection teaches the swarm to propose only what will obviously pass, and a challenger that proposes only safe changes has stopped being a challenger. A kept change whose benefit has not been realised yet reads `realisation_pending`, not zero, because scoring it as zero uplift retires an agent for being recent. | Yes |
| B-423 | 2026-09-20 | No meta-agent both proposes and judges, and opposed objectives name what they may not trade. | The author of a change has necessarily been thinking about the cases it handles well -- the failure `improve.league` refuses when a challenger brings its own tasks, here refused at the person level and at construction, not at review time. Separately, the cost optimiser and the reliability engineer pull against each other, and given one scalar score whichever runs last pays for the other out of a budget nobody agreed to spend. So each role carries `must_not_trade`, the trade vocabulary is closed so that "efficiency" cannot mean whatever a proposal needs it to mean, and no role may trade deterministic validation at all. The creative critic annotates only: a model's opinion about a design is never sole evidence for a release, and that rule does not soften because the subject is aesthetic. | Yes |
| B-424 | 2026-09-20 | The shared task set is overfitted by the league itself, so a holdout is carried and never tuned against. | Refusing a challenger's own tasks stops one author gaming one comparison and does nothing about the slower version: a hundred challengers judged over a year against the same forty tasks are selected for those forty, and nobody ever brought a task of their own. Winning the tuned set while losing the holdout is the signature of that, and it is the one result a shared-set-only comparison cannot tell from a real improvement. A holdout overlapping the shared set is refused outright -- a leaked holdout is worse than none, being the same evidence twice reported as two. A comparison with no holdout is reported as `tuned_set_only` rather than refused: a weaker claim, not an invalid one. | Yes |
| B-425 | 2026-09-20 | The promotion bar scales with how much was measured, and latency is the fourth axis. | A fixed margin treats four tasks and four hundred as the same evidence. QUALITY_MARGIN on four is within the noise of which four, and a league promoting on that churns while every report counting promotions reads it as progress; `required_margin()` therefore gets harder as the set gets smaller. It is deliberately not a t-test and does not pretend to be one -- it is a bar that moves in the direction the arithmetic has to move. Latency joined quality, cost and reliability because #180 names it and because it is the axis that looks free: nobody's line item until a cadence starts missing its window and the miss gets blamed on load. An axis that was not measured is named as unmeasured rather than read as instantaneous, and does not block a promotion. | Yes |
| B-426 | 2026-09-20 | Rollback is a recorded target with a required reason, not a retired row. | Promotion already retired the incumbent, which is not the same as being able to undo it: the moment a rollback is needed is the moment nobody can reconstruct what was running last Tuesday, and "retired_at is set on four of them" is not an answer. `rollback()` names its target, refuses one that would change which configuration is running, and requires the reason -- a rollback with no reason is indistinguishable from a second promotion, which is exactly how it reads six months later. `rollback_target()` answers what would be restored before anybody needs it, and says plainly when a first version has nothing to go back to. | Yes |
| B-427 | 2026-09-20 | Two existing league tests encoded the weaker standard and were corrected rather than the new bar relaxed. | #180's significance scaling made a 0.05 gain on the four-task fixture a coin, which failed a test written about the *cost* bar. The conflation was in the fixture, not in the new rule: a test about how cost scales should not be incidentally testing sample size, so the shared set was widened to twenty and the margin scaling given its own tests. The axis-count test moved from three to four. Recorded because relaxing a threshold until the old test passes is the obvious move and is how a standard quietly reverts. | Yes |
| B-428 | 2026-09-20 | Each department's staleness interval comes from how fast its world changes, and a cell with no declared world is refused rather than defaulted. | `improve.profiles` and `improve.bus` each carried a thirty-day constant, which is far too slow for a competitor's catalogue and meaningless for the compiler -- the arrangement #191 is written against. Four world speeds now set the interval: adversarial 24h (somebody else is actively changing it), market 72h, operational 168h. A default for an unmapped cell would quietly reintroduce the single global constant, so there is none: adding a cell to `improve.cells` without a world here raises. | Yes |
| B-429 | 2026-09-20 | Compiler mathematics has no staleness interval at all, and is answered on a code fingerprint. | A stitch count that was right in March is right in September, and re-verifying it nightly measures the clock. It goes stale when the code changes, which is a fingerprint question -- the same mechanism `ops.artefacts` uses for derived files, applied to a department. A very long interval was the easier choice and would be wrong in exactly one way: it reports the compiler current for another month after somebody changes it. A mathematical cell with no fingerprint supplied reports that it cannot say, rather than `fresh` on the strength of no date having passed. | Yes |
| B-430 | 2026-09-20 | Freshness has two clocks, and fresh-and-flat has its own name. | An SLA measured in "when did we last look" rewards looking: a department re-running a scan every hour and learning nothing scores perfectly on recency, and that is the commonest real state of an improvement programme -- busy, current and flat. #191's third clause is the antidote, so evidence age and capability movement are separate readings and `CHURNING` is reported as a problem rather than a pass. Direction is read against each cell's own polarity, because half these metrics are better when they fall and a module treating every rise as progress congratulates a department for breaking. The first version of the test for this used `finance`, whose metric is forecast *error*, and asserted a rising number was healthy -- the trap is easy to fall into from inside, which is why the polarity lives on the cell. | Yes |
| B-431 | 2026-09-20 | Never-measured is not stale, and a direction needs three measurements. | Stale evidence is re-run; absent evidence is instrumented. Filing them together produces a backlog where the second kind buries the first, and the second kind is the one nobody proposes anything about. Likewise one point is a value and two is a line through two points; three is the first number that can disagree with the ones before it, which is where `improve.profiles` already puts DECLINE_RUN. | Yes |
| B-432 | 2026-09-20 | Promotion evidence names the run that produced it, and the version it describes. | A proposal that runs its own tests and reports them passed has reported an opinion in the shape of a fact, so every piece of evidence carries a run reference and one without is refused where it is attached rather than doubted later. The second half is the one that only bites something working around the clock: a sandbox result from Tuesday describes Tuesday's proposal, and if the content moves the result is evidence about something that no longer exists. Each proposal carries a fingerprint, evidence records the fingerprint it ran against, and revising the proposal invalidates it rather than ageing it out -- the same rule as a forecast dated before its period (B-413) and a derived artefact checked against its inputs (B-360), arriving here because the version moves silently when the work is done by something that never sleeps. | Yes |
| B-433 | 2026-09-20 | Auto-promotion reads the tier and never the proposal's own view of its risk. | `PRE_AUTHORISED` is a constant, `may_auto_promote()` reads nothing the proposal says about itself, and `Proposal` has no confidence or risk field to set. Classification-by-surface exists precisely so a change cannot talk its way into a faster lane (B-178's tiers), and a self-declared risk level here would reopen that from the other side. Scope is declared when the proposal opens and enforced afterwards: a run touching a surface outside it is refused, because a bound that widens to fit what happened is a description. A surface the tier classifier does not know is refused too -- it would take no tier, and therefore no cooldown, ceiling or evidence requirement at all. | Yes |
| B-434 | 2026-09-20 | An owner card is refused unless impact, cost and rollback are each exact. | "Minimal impact, low cost, we can revert" is what an approval queue fills with when nobody checks, and a person reading it has been told nothing and will approve it -- which is how an approval step becomes a formality. Impact names surfaces, cost is a number in CAD, rollback names a concrete target; eleven vague phrasings are refused by name. An owner queued before the machine has finished its own checks is being asked to *be* the check, so a card with evidence still missing is refused as well. A pre-authorised change is never queued for a person at all: queueing one trains whoever reads the queue to approve without reading. | Yes |
| B-435 | 2026-09-20 | A nightly stage has three outcomes and never two: found, found nothing, or did not run. | The whole risk in a scheduled sweep is the word `completed`. A job that finishes without raising and writes "ok" reads identically whether it did the work or skipped every stage, and the comfortable reading is the one believed -- which is how a company reports a year of nightly improvement during which nothing was read. A boolean collapses "found nothing" into "did not run", so both exist, a skipped stage must say why, and a stage that read zero rows reports did_not_run however it describes itself: finding nothing in nothing has not established there was nothing to find. The verdict is computed from what each stage returned, so a sweep that skipped six of seven cannot report success. This build's fourth encounter with the same defect, after B-278, B-360 and B-408. | Yes |
| B-436 | 2026-09-20 | The nightly delta is machine-readable because the next night reads it, and nightly is a minimum rather than a monopoly. | A stage quiet for twenty nights and a stage quiet since yesterday are different findings, and only a comparable record separates them; `quiet_run()` reports the run length and explicitly refuses to say whether it means clean or broken, because those are the same reading. Two results for one stage are refused, since they make the night incomparable with every other. And the requirement's own last sentence is load-bearing: nightly is the *minimum* comprehensive sweep, so this queues improvements rather than promoting them and holds no lock on evidence other cadences write -- a sweep that owned the learning would make every other path wait a day. On the real empty database the handler reports `incomplete` and names the five stages with nothing to read, which is the honest output for a shadow-mode company and the one a success-on-no-exception job would hide. | Yes |
| B-437 | 2026-09-20 | Three of the four weekly architecture moves subtract, and a week that only added is asked why. | `improve.velocity` already refuses the add-only review for cadences, experiments, polish, queries and infrastructure. The argument does not stop at cadences: a review permitted to add specialists and never to merge or retire one grows the org chart every week, forever, and each addition is individually defensible -- which is exactly what makes the total indefensible and invisible. Not blocked, because a genuinely growing company exists, but not silent either. Adding a specialist for a capability a department already owns is refused outright, since that is how two roles end up doing one job. | Yes |
| B-438 | 2026-09-20 | A department may never propose the revision of its own success measure, and no revision may be argued from the old number being unflattering. | A department that changes its own metric after a bad quarter has not improved; it has moved the goalposts, and from inside it feels like better measurement because the old metric genuinely was imperfect -- every metric is. Three guards: the proposer may not be the subject, which is B-423's separation applied to the scoreboard; the old metric's history is preserved rather than migrated, because a revision that rewrites the past removes the only evidence it was self-serving; and ten phrasings of "the number is unfair" are refused by name, because the argument has to be about what the metric fails to measure. `cycle()` runs the proposer check itself rather than trusting the caller -- a rule enforced at one call site is enforced at the call sites somebody thought of. | Yes |
| B-439 | 2026-09-20 | The eight weekly domains are audited rather than visited, and nothing read is not_audited. | A weekly report of a clean bill of health across eight domains nobody looked at is the same output as one somebody did. Each domain carries the rows it read, a finding from zero rows read is refused, and the cycle will not call itself complete while any domain has no reading. On the real database several domains read zero and the cycle correctly reports incomplete. Roadmap items carried four weeks or more are separated out: an item that old is a decision nobody is making rather than work nobody has reached, and the two need opposite responses. | Yes |
| B-440 | 2026-09-20 | #190's module is `improve/upgrades.py`, because `runtime/pipeline.py` already existed. | B-421 recorded the rule -- a new module's basename is checked against the whole repository, not just its own package -- and this collision was created two hours later and found by stumbling into the other module while tracing a missing handler, not by running the check. Different packages mean no import collision and nothing would have broken; it would only have meant two unrelated modules answering to one name in a codebase where imports are read far more often than they are written. Recorded because writing a rule down is evidently not the same as following it, and the cost of catching it late was a rename across four files instead of a different first word. | Yes |
| B-441 | 2026-09-20 | A note saying a requirement is blocked does not park it; a gate does. | #133, #140 and #147 each carried a registry note stating plainly that no cultural feed is connected, and all three sat in the ready queue reporting themselves as work somebody could start. Prose in a note is read by people; the queue reads gates, and the ready count is the one number the executor exists to keep honest. A `culture_feed` gate now holds them, opening on the first CultureObservation row that names its source -- counted, not configured, and an observation with no source is the same unverifiable thing as no observation. No existing gate was right: `benchmark_observation` is about competitor listings and a search-interest feed is not that, and a gate that is nearly right opens on the wrong day, which the `owned_surfaces` gate was added for the same reason. | Yes |
| B-442 | 2026-09-20 | An owned element becomes recurring by having recurred, and there is no field that promotes it. | "Recognisable original IP that customers return for" is a claim about the second time, so it cannot be declared. Three appearances across two seasons, and eight appearances inside one season is still `proposed` -- a collection rather than IP, because returning requires having gone away. `Element` has no status field to set; status is computed from the appearances, one product cannot be counted twice, and a retired element coming back is a new proposal so the roster shows that somebody decided twice. A roster that can be populated by writing names in it measures enthusiasm. | Yes |
| B-443 | 2026-09-20 | Originality is checked over the name, the description and every primitive, using the existing rights checker. | The dangerous failure is not a product that borrows openly -- that path exists, is routed by `culture.rights`, and is recorded as borrowed. It is a character with an original name whose design is a recognisable external one: it looks like an asset, carries the full legal risk of the thing it resembles, and cannot be defended precisely because nobody wrote down what it was derived from. So `propose()` scans the whole surface rather than the name, and calls `rights.check_free_of` rather than implementing a second containment test -- two answers to "is this token present" means the more permissive one gets used, and word-boundary matching is already there so the check does not fire on 'art' inside 'heart'. An element with no recorded primitives is refused: it cannot be shown later to have been original rather than remembered. | Yes |
| B-444 | 2026-09-20 | A collection world must answer for a product it has never seen, and dependence is a direction rather than a number. | A world describing its own three products is a retrospective label for a bundle, so a world declares what it admits and excludes, `can_host()` is always asked about something new, and an undecided category is a decision to make rather than a yes -- a world that admits anything nobody has ruled out is not a setting. Separately, the requirement's goal is explicitly *not permanent dependence*, which is a direction: `dependence()` refuses to answer from a single period, because one reading cannot say whether the borrowed share is falling and falling is the requirement. Flat is reported as flat and named as not progress. | Yes |
| B-445 | 2026-09-20 | Purpose is a second axis on an asset, orthogonal to medium. | `AssetClass` says what an asset is made of -- a twin render, a photograph, an infographic. #57's own sentence asks a different question: *a technically correct chart cannot be promoted to hero merely because it rendered successfully.* Rendering successfully is a fact about the medium; being the hero is a question about the purpose, and nothing in the system held the second, which is precisely why the only reason a chart ever becomes a hero is that it was the asset that finished rendering. A stitch chart is now a DIGITAL_TWIN_RENDER by medium and ENGINEERING_EVIDENCE by purpose, and ENGINEERING_EVIDENCE cannot do DESIRE. The row that matters most in the medium/purpose table: nothing but a photograph may be PHYSICAL_PROOF, whatever it is labelled, because a label is exactly where that gets claimed. | Yes |
| B-446 | 2026-09-20 | The listing set is checked for job collision, never for image similarity. | #65's "prevent five technically different images from communicating essentially the same thing" is not about pixels, and reading it that way produces a perceptual-hash comparison that passes the actual failure: five genuinely different charts all doing DETAIL. So every frame declares exactly one job from the ten named, and two frames sharing a job is the defect. Frame one is the hero and its job is DESIRE, stated rather than derived, so a listing whose first frame documents rather than sells is refused rather than reordered -- it has spent the only frame most shoppers see. Missing jobs are listed and never refused: a small listing does not need all ten, and a shop that added a frame per uncovered job would be padding the gallery to satisfy a checklist. | Yes |
| B-447 | 2026-09-20 | Each of the four promotion gates reports passed, failed or not_run, and export requires all four to have run. | A boolean makes a gate that never ran indistinguishable from one that passed, and the version of that failure here is a listing exported because the commercial check was never wired up and therefore raised nothing. The fifth costume of the defect this build keeps meeting, after B-278, B-360, B-408 and B-435. Two verdicts for one gate are refused as well, because that lets somebody choose which to read. | Yes |
| B-448 | 2026-09-20 | The honesty label is computed from the medium, and the truth gate is consulted independently of it. | #69 says it outright -- do not use a disclaimer as permission to make a misleading hero -- and that has to be structural, because a disclaimer is the cheapest available fix for a truthfulness problem and it is always to hand. So the label is not accepted from a caller but derived from the medium, and `may_export` reads the asset-truth verdict separately: there is no ordering in which the disclaimer helps, and a labelled asset failing DATA_TRUTH still fails. It is required only where the render/photograph distinction is material -- DESIRE, DETAIL, PROOF, SCALE -- because labelling a materials list trains buyers to read nothing, which costs the label its meaning on the frames where it matters. | Yes |
| B-449 | 2026-09-20 | Every displayed measurement traces to the geometry object, and two that disagree is the headline defect. | `cir.geometry` was already canonical and already refused what it could not measure, and none of that says anything about what reached the card -- every defect #60 names happens after the measurement is right. Three checks: a number with no canonical source (it came from somewhere nobody can check and is indistinguishable from a correct one), a number with no axis or component (the requirement's own unexplained 180 cm marker, which is not wrong so much as unreadable, and unreadable is worse because nobody can disagree with it), and two displayed numbers giving different values for the same axis, component, blocked state and variant. The third is the one that matters: it is invisible to any check that validates measurements one at a time, and one at a time is how measurements are usually checked. Blocked state is mandatory because crochet changes size when blocked by more than any tolerance worth stating; size variant is required only where variants exist, since demanding the field on a single-size product trains everybody to write n/a and stop reading it. | Yes |
| B-450 | 2026-09-20 | A visual defect recurring after its fixture is a bug in the fixture, not a second bug in the renderer. | `gates.regression` gave compiler defects fixtures and visual defects never got the same treatment, for a reason worth naming: a compiler defect is a wrong number and a layout defect is a look, so it feels like taste and taste does not get a fixture. But `publish.layout_qa` measures rendered pixels, which makes these reproducible, and anything reproducible can be fixtured. A defect with no artefact that reproduces it is refused -- it is a memory, and memories prevent nothing. The recurrence distinction is what stops a second broken fixture being added instead of the first being widened. And "improve monotonically" is implemented as the narrow claim it can actually support: not that defect counts fall, because production finds what production finds, but that the set of classes with a passing fixture never shrinks. | Yes |
| B-451 | 2026-09-20 | The listing certificate is invalidated by its inputs changing, never by being revoked. | A revocation step is a step somebody forgets, and the forgetting is silent: the listing goes on carrying a certificate that was true about a version nobody sells any more. So the certificate fingerprints the geometry and claims it was issued against and `still_valid()` recomputes -- the same mechanism as B-360's artefact provenance and B-432's promotion evidence, all three asking whether a record still describes the thing it was made from. It is a separate object from `gates.certificate` because the two go stale for different reasons: a size card can be quoting geometry that has since changed while the pattern's own certificate stays perfectly valid. A geometry change affects the frames quoting measurements; a claims or policy change affects the whole set, because it is not confined to the frames showing numbers. | Yes |
| B-452 | 2026-09-20 | #61 and #64 are parked rather than left ready, on the browser_vision and a new physical_proof gate. | #61's deterministic half is already `publish.layout_qa` and `publish.eligibility`; what it leads with is independent visual review, which needs a model looking at the picture. #64's substitution half exists -- PHYSICAL_PROOF is a purpose only a photograph can serve -- and what remains is the trigger, which is a photograph of an object nobody has made. The browser_vision gate's own note already states the rule being applied: a requirement whose only remaining work needs a parked capability belongs with the parked, not in a queue advertising work nobody can start. `physical_proof` opens on the first PhysicalTest row with a completion date, counted rather than read from a flag saying testing is set up, because the intake being built is not the same as a finished object existing. | Yes |
| B-453 | 2026-09-20 | Creative may select from truth and may never extend it, checked as a subset in one direction. | #63's operative sentence is that creative may improve presentation but never invent motifs, dimensions, texture, construction or included deliverables, and the implementation is an asymmetry. Showing less than the truth is allowed and expected -- a hero depicting three of seven stitch types is a photograph of part of a thing, and no listing shows everything. Showing more is not a presentation improvement: it is a claim about a pattern that does not contain it, made by an image nobody thought of as a statement, and the buyer who counted on it was told something untrue. A category the brief is silent about is refused, because silence is a category creative may fill in; an empty allowance is a real constraint and stays distinct from no entry at all. | Yes |
| B-454 | 2026-09-20 | The evidence-to-creative flow is ordered, and a brief produced after the creative is a caption. | A creative brief written once the conversion creative exists describes it rather than constraining it, which is the same shape as a forecast dated after its period (B-413) and arrives for the same reason: the convenient order is the wrong one and nothing else notices. So stages advance one at a time and a skip names what would be missed. Going backwards is permitted and reported as *restarting* rather than continuing, because a defect found at the gates genuinely does send work back to the brief and the evidence downstream is now about an older version. And the asset-truth comparison is refused when its author made the creative: the requirement says independent, and the author of a picture has been thinking about what it does well -- the failure `improve.league` refuses when a configuration brings its own tasks. | Yes |
| B-455 | 2026-09-20 | The first three frames are a context, not a prefix. | A phone gallery shows three before anybody scrolls, and most people do not scroll, so those three answer the buying question between them or nothing does -- and an excellent gallery below them is invisible. Three frames all doing one job is one frame shown three times, in the only context most shoppers occupy. The wider premise: a listing is assembled at full size, one frame at a time, by somebody who already knows what the product is, and chosen at 170 pixels in a grid of competitors by somebody who does not, so a check that runs only at build size runs in a context no shopper is ever in. A thin gallery is reported without being a defect, because two good frames are not a failure and the empty space is still worth seeing. | Yes |
| B-456 | 2026-09-20 | A mobile QA pass with no stored render is not a pass. | "We checked the mobile view" is not a finding anybody can revisit when a listing underperforms three months later, which is exactly when somebody wants to. Every context result carries the render that produced it, and an unrendered context reads `not_rendered` rather than passed -- the same distinction as B-435's nightly stages and B-447's promotion gates. Two renders of one context are refused as well, because that lets somebody pick which to look at. | Yes |
| B-457 | 2026-09-20 | #168 and #79 are parked on benchmark_purchases and browser_vision. | Both registry notes already said what they waited for -- #168 "unrunnable until benchmarks are purchased", #79 "needs the vision capability" -- and both sat in the ready queue. The third time this session that prose in a note was doing a gate's job (B-441, B-452). Ready is now 15 against 87 parked, and the ready count means what it says. | Yes |
| B-458 | 2026-09-20 | Seasonal priority comes from a score with no favourites, and the factors multiply. | #33's merge instruction is to preserve Christmas as the current campaign rather than the company identity, and `compression.PRIORITY_PROGRAMMES` is `{"Christmas": 0.45}` -- a constant naming one occasion, granting it nearly half of engineering capacity permanently, read by five modules. That is the Christmas Strike Team in code, and no amount of rolling-wave machinery around it changes what it says. The seven factors multiply rather than average because they are conjunctive: an occasion with no time remaining scores zero however strong its demand, and an average lets six good factors carry one fatal one -- the same reason `scale.confidence` takes a minimum over its rungs. | B-459 | 2026-09-20 | The evergreen floor is the inverse of the priority floor, and it is taken before anything is granted. | `MIN_PRIORITY_SHARE` stops a seasonal programme decaying to a rounding error; nothing stopped evergreen being squeezed to zero by an aggressive seasonal allocation, and a shop entirely inside Christmas has nothing to sell in February, which is exactly when it needs something. | B-463 | 2026-09-20 | The Christmas constant is replaced by a labelled accessor, and the seed survives because the evidence does not. | All five call sites now read `compression.priority_shares()`, which returns scores from `seasonal.engine` when any occasion has them and the owner's named campaign, labelled `current_campaign_seed`, when none does. | B-464 | 2026-09-20 | Parity alone is refused as a reason to enter an arena, because the failure is aggregation rather than any single decision. | Every individual choice to match the benchmark is defensible -- they are good, this is what good looks like, we should be at least this good -- and a year of defensible choices is a shop that looks like a copy of a shop. | B-479 | 2026-09-20 | No gate in the executor's table opens on a typed string any more, and a test asserts it over the whole table. | Three did: `browser_vision` read `BRAMBLELOOP_BROWSER_URL`, `image_generation` read `BRAMBLELOOP_IMAGE_KEY`, `offsite_storage` read `BRAMBLELOOP_ARCHIVE_URL`. Re-confirmed today from this environment's own address: `robots.txt` answers 200 and `/legal/terms` answers 403 to a client identifying itself honestly. B-268 already decided what follows: getting past that by pretending to be a browser is evasion, and doing it to a marketplace this company intends to sell on trades a small convenience for the relationship. Residential proxying and stealth fingerprinting are the same trade with a subscription attached. So a worker running Playwright inside the existing Railway project would cost perhaps CA$1-3 a month and would most likely return 403s, and a hosted browser API at US$50+ a month would buy the same 403 more expensively. The client and its probe are built and committed; what is *not* bought is a service, and the recommendation is to leave it unbought until either Etsy grants access to the surfaces or a different surface answers the same questions. A capability that cannot be bought is a more useful finding than a capability nobody costed. | Yes |
| B-501 | 2026-09-20 | Ten benchmark purchases cover every department but two; thirteen cover everything, for CA$56 more. | Run against the 438 observed listings: ten picks total CA$212 and leave `stockings`, `seasonal_gift` and `unclassified` unbought. Those are not arbitrary -- stockings and seasonal gifts are the two departments the current campaign window is aimed at -- so the marginal CA$56 buys the departments this company most intends to sell into, which is the kind of thing a coverage objective surfaces and a popularity sort would have buried. The selection is deterministic and reproducible, and every pick names the distinct unknown it answers, so the owner is reviewing reasoning rather than a list. | Yes |
| B-502 | 2026-09-20 | The image generator is chosen by measurement, and the standing recommendation was deleted so the benchmark has nothing to confirm. | The owner's decision is explicit: do not lock a provider because it is inexpensive, and a moderately dearer model that materially outperforms is worth paying for. There was a recommendation in the code -- the cheapest candidate that supports reference conditioning -- and leaving it there with an unrun benchmark beside it would have been the price list deciding again with a second opinion for cover. `state()` now returns `recommended: None` and says why, and a test asserts there is no default. The arithmetic supports the instruction rather than arguing with it: the whole monthly spread across the four eligible models is CA$2.19 to CA$6.90, which is not an amount any catalogue decision should turn on, and a test fails if that spread ever grows enough to make cost a real argument. | Yes |
| B-503 | 2026-09-20 | One candidate leaves on a requirement rather than on a score, and it is the one with the best published photorealism. | Imagen 4 Ultra is described by its own publisher as the hardest output to distinguish from a photograph. It has no reference conditioning on that tier, and an identity lock (#200, #201) is reference conditioning rather than a better prompt -- so the canonical model cannot be held across seasons by it at any quality. Excluding it before the benchmark rather than letting it win and then discarding the result is the honest order, and it is the clearest case in this build of a capability being genuinely excellent and genuinely unable to meet the requirement. | Yes |
| B-504 | 2026-09-20 | Five samples per trial, not three, and not as many as the ceiling would allow. | One render is a sample of a distribution: a benchmark taking one measures luck and reports it as capability. Five gives each rubric dimension twenty-five observations per model, enough to separate a reliably good generator from an occasionally spectacular one -- which is the distinction that matters for a catalogue somebody ships every week. Three would have cost CA$2.50 less and left the decision closer to the noise, on a choice that governs every listing image this company makes. Stopping at five rather than spending the remaining CA$18 of headroom is the other half of the owner's principle: budget is not a reason, and more samples past the point where they change the answer buy nothing. | Yes |
| B-505 | 2026-09-20 | The benchmark's judge is blind, is not the generator, and every rubric line cites a requirement. | A judge told which model rendered an image grades the brand; a generator asked to grade itself has never failed. So each render is scored by the vision capability with no knowledge of its origin, against seven dimensions each carrying the requirement it comes from -- stitch fidelity and material truth from #79, finished-result clarity and lifestyle quality from #75, thumbnail strength from #66, product-as-subject from #202, identity match from #201. A rubric line with no requirement behind it is somebody's taste, and taste is what this build refuses to decide with. The trials render original Brambleloop subjects: benchmarking on a competitor's product would be commissioning a copy to see how good the copier is. | Yes |
| B-506 | 2026-09-20 | A benchmark with no results names nobody, and a model below the fabric floor is out at any price. | Two refusals, both guarding the same failure: a measurement quietly becoming a price comparison. With no scores, `decide()` returns `decided: false` rather than falling back to the cheapest candidate -- which is precisely the outcome the owner ruled out, and exactly what a resultless benchmark becomes if nobody writes the refusal. And a generator that cannot render crochet fabric convincingly is not a candidate for a crochet shop whatever its totals, so the two fabric dimensions have a floor that disqualifies rather than merely lowering an average. Cost breaks a tie only inside the margin five samples can separate, and it is expressed as a score margin rather than a dollar rule, because at these prices any dollar rule would dominate. | Yes |
| B-507 | 2026-09-20 | Each benchmark purchase now carries what it beat, and the set carries how much of the catalogue it makes redundant. | The owner asked why each listing was selected instead of another MJs product, and a coverage score is a number rather than an answer. Each pick now names the runner-up, what that alternative would have taught instead, and the extra the winner adds. The set-level number is the one that answers "are we buying thirteen similar things": how many of the 438 observed listings would now teach nothing the set does not already teach. Measured over the finished set rather than per pick, because the per-pick count reads zero in a catalogue of near-duplicates right up until the duplicate is bought, which is the opposite of informative. | Yes |
| B-508 | 2026-09-20 | #268 is data-gated, and the Etsy capability it briefly waited on is recorded as satisfied evidence. | The owner's instruction is not to request an owner action that has already been performed. The sanctioned read credential was proven by use on 2026-09-19 and read the 438 listings the mission runs on; #268 spent part of a day parked on the gate naming it, which was a condition already true (B-482). What remains is a comparison against a second buyer market, which is evidence this company does not hold rather than a decision the owner owes -- so `data_gated`, the same classification #140 takes for the same reason, and the gate opens by itself the day a second shop is observed on the credential that already exists. | Yes |
| B-509 | 2026-09-20 | Eleven of the first thirteen purchases were decided by a stable sort, which is deterministic and meaningless. | Reading the real run against 438 listings rather than the tests: after the first two picks, every runner-up was *level* on coverage, so the reason recorded for each was "it sorted first". Once a department was claimed, every other listing in it was worth exactly the same to the objective -- which is the objective admitting it had run out of things to distinguish, on precisely the instruction the owner gave about maximum information diversity. Two fixes. `has_video` becomes a facet, because whether a listing ships a video alongside the PDF is a real difference in what the customer receives and therefore in what a teardown can study. And ties break on how much of the customer experience is visible on the page -- gallery depth, video, stated deliverable terms, stated sizing -- counted rather than weighted, since pretending a video and two extra photographs convert into each other would be a second arbitrary choice wearing arithmetic. The tie-break fires late by construction: early on a richer listing usually *adds* a facet and wins on coverage outright, and it is once the catalogue's variety is covered that coverage stops discriminating. That is exactly where the eleven were. | Yes |
| B-510 | 2026-09-20 | The observable-depth tie-break discriminated once in thirteen against the real catalogue, because almost every MJs listing carries exactly ten images. | Ten is Etsy's gallery cap, so `gallery_depth` reads "rich" almost everywhere and a tie-break built on it is nearly constant on the data it was written for. That is the second mechanism in two hours that looked sound and could not separate the cases it existed for -- the first could not fire at all (its inputs were the same facets it was breaking ties on), and this one fires and says nothing. Both were found by reading a live run rather than by a test, and neither test could have caught it: a synthetic fixture makes the variety the real catalogue does not have. Worth recording as a habit rather than two incidents: a discriminator has to be checked against the distribution it will actually see, and "does this fire" is a different question from "does this separate". | Yes |
| B-511 | 2026-09-20 | Popularity breaks a tie inside a department and never chooses the set, and the difference is the whole argument. | This module exists partly to refuse the popularity sort: taking the ten most-favourited listings buys ten similar things, because in one shop's catalogue they share a department, a price band and a format. That argument is about the *set objective* and it stands. Inside a department the set has already decided to cover on diversity grounds, popularity changes nothing about diversity -- it picks the most instructive exemplar of a slot chosen on other grounds, and a department's strongest seller is the customer experience most worth studying (#317 buys these to study exactly that). A test asserts it never displaces a department. Absent favourites read as zero and lose a tie rather than winning one, because a listing nobody has audited must not read as unpopular *or* as popular. | Yes |
| B-512 | 2026-09-20 | The approved research budget is a ceiling in code, and a purchase the ceiling forces is recorded as a swap rather than presented as a choice. | Once popularity broke the department ties, the strongest `collections` exemplar became an CA$85 ebook and the thirteen-pattern set came to CA$309 against the CA$300 approved. A ceiling nobody enforces is a number somebody remembers at the till -- the same rule the model gateway already follows, applied to a research purchase. The selector now takes the best exemplar the remaining budget allows and records what it could not afford, with the difference in dollars, so "we bought the cheaper one" is visible rather than silent. Reporting the forgone option matters more than the swap: the owner's standing instruction is to surface the stronger option when modest extra spend buys materially more, and a selector that quietly fitted the budget would be making exactly the trade it was told not to make on its own. | Yes |
| B-513 | 2026-09-20 | The governing spend policy is QUALITY FIRST, COST SECOND, WASTE NEVER, at a CA$100 monthly ceiling, and the three clauses are ordered. | The owner raised the combined model, vision and image ceiling from CA$25 and reversed the argument that went with it. The build had followed "start economically" faithfully and the faithfulness had become the problem: routing choices, cadence intervals and analysis depths had accumulated whose stated justification was the ceiling rather than the work -- each defensible on its own, and together a company optimising for cheapness. Recorded in `finance/spend_policy.py` as code rather than as prose, with the six spending priorities the owner named and, beside each, what may not be traded away for money. `may_downgrade_for_cost` refuses the declared trade, which does not stop anybody hard-coding a cheaper model -- nothing could -- but means a downgrade has to be made without saying why, and a change with no stated reason is what review is for. Waste stays refused at any budget, because waste is not a saving that was declined; it is spending with nothing on the other side of it. | Yes |
| B-514 | 2026-09-20 | The ceiling is written once and a test refuses a second literal. | It was written in two modules and a production assertion: `routing.MONTHLY_CEILING_CAD`, `anthropic.DEFAULT_MONTHLY_CEILING_CAD`, and `check("model_spend_within_its_ceiling", ceiling <= 25.0 ...)` in `/api/verify`. The third is the instructive one -- a hardcoded bound inside a safety check turns red the moment an owner decision is carried out, which is the exact shape of check the file's own comment three lines above warns against, added by the session that wrote the warning. The same applies to the CA$25 benchmark budget, now also in the policy file: owner-authorised money is written once, in the file that says what the authority is. | Yes |
| B-515 | 2026-09-20 | Four vision call sites bypassed the routing table entirely, and one of them was the owner's second spending priority. | `routing.TASKS` declared `gallery_observation` on the standard tier. `intel.vision` constructed its provider with `VISION_PROBE_MODEL` -- the *probe's* model, chosen because a probe should be the smallest possible real call -- so MJs gallery analysis ran on the cheapest tier while the table said otherwise, for a day. The same in `creative.reference` (the construction evidence a concept brief is written from), `visual.inspect` (a release-blocking realism judgement) and the image benchmark's own judge. A table nothing reads is documentation. Fixed with `provider_for(task)`, three new declared tasks, and two tests: every vision call site names a task in the table, and no module outside the gateway names a model string at all. A probe's model is not an analysis model, and the two sharing a constant is how the substitution happened without anybody choosing it. | Yes |
| B-516 | 2026-09-20 | Two cadence intervals were reconciled and one deliberately was not, and the difference is whether money was the reason. | The blinded creative benchmark ran monthly with the stated reason that weekly would be 27% of a CA$25 ceiling; that argument is gone, and it is now fortnightly rather than weekly -- because the thing being tracked does not move in seven days, and sampling faster than the signal changes buys noise and a larger bill. It stops where more frequency would stop adding information rather than where it would start costing money, which is the distinction the policy turns on. Gallery analysis went from ten images every four hours to twenty-five every two: the old number closed the benchmark's visual evidence in nineteen days and was set by the ceiling, and MJs analysis depth is named as something not to reduce for cost. It is safe to raise because it is self-limiting -- once the backlog empties the handler judges only new and changed listings, and a rate that stayed high against an empty queue would be the waste clause rather than the quality one. The weekly arena expedition keeps its interval: the constraint on how often widening the catalogue is worth doing is how fast the gap queue refills, not what it costs. | Yes |
| B-517 | 2026-09-20 | Every billable call now carries provider, model, agent, department, product and purpose as columns, with the reservation beside the bill. | They lived in a JSON blob, written where somebody remembered, so "what did the money produce" was a question nobody could answer with a query -- and a spend review that cannot be queried becomes a total and an anecdote. `estimated_cad` is the other half: an estimate nobody compares against the invoice can drift by a factor of three, and did, when the deep tier was priced at a third of its rate for a whole session and every ceiling check in that session was computed against the wrong number without one of them failing. Variance is reported per purpose, and under-estimating is flagged specifically, because that is the direction where the ceiling is being checked against a number smaller than the bill. Unattributed rows keep their dollars: dropping them would show a tidier number that does not match the invoice, and the gap would be exactly the spending nobody could account for. | Yes |
| B-518 | 2026-09-20 | The spend report covers the calendar month rather than "the month so far". | Its upper bound was `now`, which silently dropped any row timestamped a few seconds ahead of the reader's clock: two processes, two clocks, and spend that exists and does not appear. A report that hides money because of skew is the same fault as one that drops unattributed rows, wearing correctness as a costume. Found by a test whose fixture wrote a row at real time and read it as of a fixed `now`. Elapsed time for the burn rate is still measured to `now`; what changed is which rows count. | Yes |
| B-519 | 2026-09-20 | The image benchmark scores twelve judged dimensions, asks gallery consistency once per set, and measures repeatability and latency rather than judging them. | The owner named thirteen criteria. Nine are per-image judgements and are in the rubric with the requirement each comes from. Gallery consistency is a property of a *set* -- six images each individually fine can still read as six unrelated stock photographs -- so it is asked once per model over that model's own renders. Repeatability and latency are computed from data the run already produces: a model asked to rate its own consistency is answering a different question, and repeatability is the spread of its own scores across five samples of the same trial. Cost is reported and used only to break a tie. Two floors disqualify rather than lowering an average: fabric-and-construction, because a generator that cannot render crochet is not a candidate for a crochet shop at any price, and identity, because a face that does not survive one regeneration produces a catalogue that drifts into somebody else by February. | Yes |
| B-520 | 2026-09-20 | Inside the deciding margin, repeatability breaks the tie before cost does. | Both are tie-breaks and only one of them is about quality. A model that is sometimes excellent and sometimes poor is worse to ship with than one that is consistently good, because a catalogue goes out weekly and nobody re-rolls the bad frame. Cost is the last key in the sort, which is the policy stated as a sort key rather than as a paragraph -- and the sort key is the part that still holds when nobody is reading the paragraph. Judging the benchmark moved to the deep tier at the same time, which took it from CA$0.48 to CA$12.84 of the CA$25 authorised: the judgement decides which provider renders every listing image afterwards, so measuring carefully with a blunt instrument was the one economy that could not be defended. | Yes |
| B-521 | 2026-09-20 | The image-token estimate was wrong in the dangerous direction twice, and the reconciliation the owner asked for caught it within the hour. | It was 800, derived from the provider's (width x height)/750 rule against Etsy's 570-wide gallery variant. The first production probe measured 1,562 for one image, because the observer stores `il_fullxfull` -- the full-size original, not the thumbnail the arithmetic assumed -- so it was raised to 2,000, which looked like room above the measurement. The first real analysis run then billed 126,518 input tokens for 25 images: about 5,000 an image, two and a half times the corrected figure. One probe measured one small image and generalised; the catalogue's galleries are larger than the one that happened to be first. Both errors were the same shape -- an estimate derived from a rule or a sample rather than from the workload -- and both went under, which is the direction that turns a ceiling into a suggestion. Set to 6,000 from the measured mean with a fifth above it. Three ways of being wrong about this number have now been tried and the one that worked was measuring the actual work, which is the whole argument for keeping the reservation beside the bill. | Yes |
| B-522 | 2026-09-20 | Gallery analysis stops at 40% of the month, and the cap exists for priority order rather than thrift. | At the measured CA$0.029 an image the raised cadence is CA$8.70 a day: CA$33 to drain the 1,126-image backlog over four days, and CA$260 a month if the queue never empties. The queue does empty -- the handler judges only new and changed listings afterwards -- and a capability whose safety depends on an assumption about a queue has no guard at all. The reason for the cap is the owner's own priority order: product creativity is first and MJs intelligence second, so a four-day image backlog must not consume the month and leave concept generation refused at the ceiling. It returns rather than raising, because the caller is a drain loop and a loop that crashes on a budget boundary loses the work it had already done; and when it stops it names the constrained work, which is the escalation path rather than a quiet downgrade. | Yes |
| B-523 | 2026-09-20 | Twenty-four of the first twenty-five ledger rows came back `unattributed`, and the report said so rather than tidying them away. | The accounting columns landed with a single writer, and three older paths -- the model probe, the vision probe and the image probe -- still wrote bare CostEntry rows with the purpose buried in a JSON blob. The live report showed it immediately: CA$1.21 of CA$1.93 attributed to nothing, and 24 calls with no reservation recorded. That is exactly what the `unattributed` bucket is for, and it is the argument for keeping it: a report that dropped those rows would have shown a tidier number that did not match the bill, and nobody would have gone looking. All three now write through the accounting path. | Yes |

## Standing constraints carried from the spec

These are not re-decidable by a future session without the owner:

- Patterns are software releases. Never generate a beauty image and ask a model to guess the
  instructions (section 2).
- No fake reviews, buyers, favourites, sock-puppets, deceptive discounts or artificial
  engagement (Execution Directive, section 8).
- Never bypass CAPTCHA, KYC, identity checks or legal acceptance.
- Never copy competitor instructions, charts, photography or protected designs. Research is
  for demand and merchandising intelligence only.
- Never claim an integration, deployment, pattern, listing, test, campaign, customer or
  revenue exists until verified.
- Deterministic pattern validation wins even if every LLM disagrees (section 27).
| B-524 | 2026-09-21 | The off-provider archive is a separate job from the continuity proof, and an unconfigured destination is recorded as a failed archive rather than skipped. | `ops.continuity` proves the export restores; `ops.offsite_archive` proves a copy of it exists somewhere losing Railway would not reach. They are separate questions, and one job reporting one verdict would let a healthy local restore stand in for an archive that was never written. The skip is the more interesting half: the gate reads these audit rows, so a handler that returned early on a missing variable would leave the last `ok` standing from whenever the credential last worked -- the same shape as every other defect this build has found, a fact recorded once surviving the world changing. Pruning runs only after a good write, because ageing out the oldest copy on the day the newest upload failed is the one moment it must not happen. | Yes |
| B-525 | 2026-09-21 | Image credentials are held per account, and the benchmark renders each candidate through its own provider. | `image_bench.run` called `images.generate` with no provider, which resolves whichever provider `BRAMBLELOOP_IMAGE_PROVIDER` names. Every candidate's prompt would have gone to one endpoint. It would not have failed -- it would have returned five near-identical rows and handed the decision to the cost tie-break, which is exactly the decision the owner ruled out on 2026-09-20. Per *account* rather than per model because that is how the credential is issued: one Google AI Studio key renders both Google candidates, and two variables holding the same secret is a secret that gets rotated in one of them. A candidate with no credential is now recorded as unmeasured, never rendered by somebody else. | Yes |
| B-526 | 2026-09-21 | A benchmark winner chosen while an eligible candidate went unmeasured is provisional, not a lock. | The owner's rule is that the cheap candidate must not be chosen because it is cheap. That rule is broken exactly as thoroughly by a run in which the dearer candidates had no key as by a decision made from a price list, and the second one looks like a measurement. `decide` reports `locked: false` and names what was missing. | Yes |
| B-527 | 2026-09-21 | `plan()["runnable"]` is computed from credentials rather than written `False`. | It was a literal `False` with a sentence beside it explaining that no provider account existed. That was true when it was written and would have gone on being reported after the accounts were created. The same defect as the parked-on sentences of B-480 and the `vision_available=False` default of B-492, in a third costume. It now reads which candidates hold a credential, and `blocked_on` names the sign-ups and what each unlocks rather than asserting that creating one is impossible. | Yes |
| B-528 | 2026-09-21 | The purchase selector records why its loop ended, rather than inferring it from the length of the list. | On the live catalogue at the approved CA$300 the set stops at twelve with the `education` department uncovered, and the report said "no remaining listing adds a facet nothing selected already covers". It was the budget, by CA$13. The two causes need different answers from the owner -- one is settled by raising the ceiling and the other is not -- so reporting the wrong one is worse than reporting none. The excluded pick, its price, its shortfall and what it would have taught are now carried in `budget_excluded`. | Yes |
| B-529 | 2026-09-21 | Purchased benchmark files are mirrored to the off-provider archive, encrypted, and never pruned. | Railway replaces this host's filesystem on every deploy, so a purchase uploaded and not mirrored is a purchase that will have to be made again -- and thirteen purchases is CA$292 of the owner's money. They go up sealed with a key the bucket credentials do not include, because backing up somebody else's copyrighted files to a third-party bucket in the clear is a different decision from backing them up. `offsite.prune` works from the continuity archive's own audit rows and never sees these keys: a purchase is not a snapshot to age out. Intake reports `durable: false` with the reason when no destination is configured, because "uploaded" and "kept" are different claims and the honest one is the one the owner needs. | Yes |
| B-530 | 2026-09-21 | The promise-to-delivery audit has three outcomes, and an unobserved promise is `unverifiable` rather than `kept`. | Intake compares what the listing said a buyer receives against what the files show, from filenames only. The temptation is two outcomes, and both defaults are wrong: resolving an unobserved claim to `kept` reports a clean bill of health for a purchase nobody looked at, and resolving it to `missing` manufactures a finding about the seller out of a gap in our own observation. It stores its inputs rather than its verdict, so a re-observed listing produces a re-computed comparison instead of a stale one. | Yes |
| B-531 | 2026-09-21 | An owner action whose requirement is no longer requested closes itself, and the closing is guarded against an assessment that produced nothing. | `launch.readiness` added actions and restated their figures and never closed one, so the queue only ever grew. A request stops being generated the moment its requirement is met, but its row stayed open for ever -- which makes the oldest entries the ones most likely to be finished, and the live queue was asking the owner for the Etsy shop that exists, the developer app that is working and the model key that has spent CA$2.66. The owner's standing instruction is explicit: do not ask for an action already completed. The guard matters as much as the fix: "not requested" and "not assessed" are indistinguishable from the closing code, and an assessment that yields no requests at all is far more likely to be a failure than a company with nothing left for its owner to do. Closing on that would erase the record of everything ever asked. | Yes |
| B-532 | 2026-09-21 | The calibration sample is withdrawn from the owner queue and recorded as parked by the owner; the requirement is unchanged and still blocks. | The owner has said twice that they will not be the one who crochets it. Those are two different claims and the honest state needs both: yardage remains an uncalibrated estimate carrying plus or minus 20%, still blocking calibrated claims and every fitted garment -- and the ask is gone. A queue that keeps asking for the one thing its reader has refused teaches its reader to stop opening the queue, which costs the other nine rows. `PHYSICAL_SAMPLE` is kept rather than deleted, because a park is reversible and the wording it would be reversed to should not have to be rewritten from memory. The other way through is named in the requirement's evidence: a pattern tester, which is `tester_roster` and is outreach rather than an owner action. | Yes |
| B-533 | 2026-09-21 | The benchmark-purchase ask points at the selection and the upload page, at the approved CA$300. | It asked for "about ten representative competitor patterns" dropped "in its own folder under the benchmark library path", at an estimated CA$120. All three parts were written before the catalogue, the selector and the phone page existed: there is no folder on a phone, the set is chosen for facet coverage rather than approximated at ten, and CA$120 was a guess where CA$292 is the cost of a named list. An owner action that describes a workflow the system no longer has is a worse failure than a missing one, because it will be attempted. | Yes |
| B-534 | 2026-09-21 | Google AI Studio requires billing for image output, and the owner action list said the opposite. | Checked against Google's own pricing page rather than remembered: Nano Banana 2's image output reads "Not available" under Free Tier, so generation is paid-tier only. The accounts table said `needs_card: False` and the credential plan therefore sorted Google first as the free, two-minute action -- which would have produced a key that authenticates and refuses to render. "Free to create" and "free to use" are different claims and the owner was handed the wrong one, in the field whose entire job is to say what an action costs before it is taken. | Yes |
| B-535 | 2026-09-21 | Nano Banana 2 is priced at the resolution the benchmark actually renders. | US$0.063 was roughly Google's 1K figure (1,120 tokens at US$60 per million, US$0.067). This candidate is benchmarked at 2048px, which is 1,680 tokens and US$0.101 -- so the table carried an estimate for a rendering nobody was going to make, and understated the dearest candidate by 60%. The same direction as every previous pricing error in this build: under the truth, which is the direction that turns a ceiling into a suggestion. The benchmark's plan moves from CA$18.55 to CA$20.11 of the approved CA$25. | Yes |
| B-536 | 2026-09-21 | Imagen 4 is removed from the provider table and kept only as a recorded exclusion. | It was listed to be ruled out on the requirement rather than on taste -- no reference conditioning, so #200 and #201 are unmeetable by it whatever its photorealism. As of 2026-09-21 it no longer appears on Google's Gemini API pricing page at all. A provider table is what the generator can be *pointed at*, and a row for a model nobody sells is a row that will one day be selected and then fail as a credential problem rather than as what it is. The exclusion survives in `image_bench.CANDIDATES`, where it is a decision rather than an endpoint. | Yes |
| B-537 | 2026-09-21 | Each provider gets the request its API actually accepts, and the first real key is what proved it. | `generate` sent one invented body -- `{"prompt", "size"}` with a bearer token -- to every provider's base endpoint, on the stated reasoning that the differences "are not worth an abstraction nobody has exercised". That reasoning collapsed the moment a credential existed: Google wants `x-goog-api-key`, a `:generateContent` suffix, a `contents` array and an `imageConfig`, and would have refused that body under any billing arrangement. An abstraction nobody has exercised is not thin, it is untested. Verified live: Google's shape earned a quota error rather than a parse error, Black Forest Labs' earned a 402 rather than a 404 or a 401, and both FLUX 2 Pro and GPT Image 2 then rendered real images once funded. | Yes |
| B-538 | 2026-09-21 | An empty balance is `QuotaUnavailable`, not a rate limit and not a content refusal. | Two live responses on the same evening. Google answered 429 `generate_content_free_tier_requests, limit: 0` -- a rate limit says "slower" and a free-tier limit of zero says "not on this plan", and the generic mapping would have had the queue back off and retry a request that could never succeed while reporting a transient outage. Black Forest Labs answered 402 `Insufficient credits`, which fell through to `ImagesRefused`, whose own message says a content refusal "is a fact about the brief rather than the wiring" -- so the audit log would have recorded the provider declining to render a crochet basket on content grounds. A wrong diagnosis is worse than none, because somebody acts on it: a content refusal is answered with a new brief and this is answered with a top-up. A candidate that cannot pay is recorded unmeasured once rather than failing thirty identical trials. | Yes |
| B-539 | 2026-09-21 | Every render is written to this disk, because a presigned link is only an image while it lasts. | Black Forest Labs returns a URL that expires about ten minutes after it is issued: measured, issued 01:47Z with `se=2026-09-21T01:57:00Z`. Passing it along works for a benchmark that judges immediately and fails quietly everywhere else -- a model whose thirty renders outlast the expiry loses its earliest images before they are scored, and the canonical identity pack of #200, the frozen reference every future listing is conditioned on, cannot be a set of links that stop resolving over lunch.
