The obvious convenience is refused: `brief.approved_reference` returns the *pre-revision* body frames, which are the superseded pack the owner replaced, so a silent fallback to them would enforce the wrong body while reporting a pass -- exactly the automatic selection the approval ruled out. With no recoverable body reference the floor reads `unverifiable`, which is what it honestly is. | Yes |
| B-633 | 2026-09-22 | A model-bearing listing asset is a two-frame sequence, because the body floor and the fabric floor are questions about two different photographs. | The first correctly-framed live attempt produced the finding. The three-quarter shot showed the whole body and the motif judge read its fabric as `unmeasurable`, naming what it needed: "a closer or flatter frame". At 1024 pixels a hat occupies a few hundred of them and no stitch in it can be counted; pulling in far enough to count stitches loses the hips. The two floors are not in tension because either is wrong -- they are questions about different pictures, which is what a listing gallery has always been, and #75 asks for "a complete purposeful sequence without filler". This is the smallest sequence that is complete: one frame showing the garment on a body and one showing the fabric it is made of. Each floor is taken from the frame in a position to answer it, so a close crop's `unmeasurable` hips do not drag the body floor down; the shared floors take the worst answer any frame gave; and a `fail` seen anywhere blocks everywhere, because both frames ship and being the wrong authority is not a licence. | Yes |
| B-634 | 2026-09-22 | An image sent to a provider is labelled by its bytes, never by its filename. | The artifact store is content-addressed, so its files are named for their sha256 and carry no extension at all -- and `mimetypes.guess_type` returns nothing for such a name. `_image_block` defaulted that to `image/png`: a guess wearing a fact's clothes. The canonical portrait is a JPEG. Recovered from the store as an extensionless file and sent labelled `image/png`, it was refused by the provider and the face comparison came back `unmeasurable` on all five dimensions -- while the body reference, which happens to be a PNG, worked. Half the identity check silently did not run and the record showed a clean `unverifiable` rather than an error, which is worse than it failing, because "nobody could tell" and "the call was rejected" are different findings with different fixes. Sniffing the magic numbers is now authoritative and the filename is only the fallback; a file whose bytes say nothing recognisable is refused rather than assumed. A value living in two places disagrees with itself, and here one of the two is evidence. | Yes |
| B-635 | 2026-09-22 | Styling is read off the fit frame rather than asked of every frame. | Five of the character bible's fourteen questions are about wardrobe, and a frame cropped to a hat answers none of them -- so the first live sequence returned `styling: unjudged` on the detail frame, making a sixth floor unclearable by the same mistake the morphology floor had just been rescued from, one floor over and within the hour. The fit frame shows the whole styling, so the fit frame is asked. A styling *failure* seen in any frame still blocks, because both frames ship. | Yes |
| B-636 | 2026-09-22 | The photographic-realism judge is asked, once per version of its checks, whether it can pass a photograph nobody generated. | Two renders in a row were blocked on `skin_looks_real`, `processing_is_restrained` and `not_sterile_perfection`, and the second of them plainly had pores, freckles and fine lines in it. At that point there are two possibilities and they need opposite fixes: the renders really are unphotographic, or the judge cannot pass a photograph. A standard nothing can clear is the same defect as a floor nothing can fail, and this system has found that shape five times now -- so it is a question to ask rather than the flattering answer to assume, and asking it is the only thing that makes "tighten the render" a justified next move rather than chasing something nothing can meet. The control is a real listing photograph from the observed benchmark: a photograph a real person took with a real camera. It is a control and nothing else -- not copied, not re-hosted, not imitated, and what it depicts is never described, which keeps it on the right side of the rule against using a competitor's expression. Both answers are recorded as findings; neither is a failure. | Yes |
| B-637 | 2026-09-22 | The eight-part parity gate is computed before the phase check and enforced after every capability check. | The split is the whole design. Computed first because Shadow Mode refuses every publication, so a gate enforced behind the phase check would reach its first real listing having executed zero times, and the first listing it ever judged would be a live one -- the exact maturity gap between implemented and exercised that this build keeps having to take requirements back over. It reads evidence already on file, with no render, no model call and no network, so running it on every refused attempt costs nothing and produces the only record of it working that Shadow Mode permits. Enforced after the phase, the credentials and the authority matrix, because those ask whether this system may publish at all and must stay outermost: a system that refuses to publish for a creative reason before establishing that it is allowed to publish would have inverted its own gates, and the refusal `/api/verify` counts as evidence of Shadow Mode working would quietly have started saying something else. COMPETITIVE reads `unjudged`, which blocks, and that is the honest state: gallery-level vision analysis is absent on all 438 observed benchmark listings, so no per-listing imagery comparison exists to consult. | Yes |
| B-638 | 2026-09-22 | The character bible is enforced as a floor, and its rejected drift examples are read out of the record rather than composed. | #73 names makeup range, expression range, wardrobe rules and lighting language, and the pack carried none of them; the rules that existed lived in `brief.py` as prompt language -- asked for, never verified, which is the same defect as a gate reading configuration instead of demonstrated capability. The identity gate would accept exactly the right woman in heavy editorial makeup under coloured rim light wearing a printed dress that competes with the crochet, because every dimension it measures would still match, and #202 is the requirement that says such a frame is wrong. So the four parts are written as fourteen closed questions about the rendered frame, answered by a vision model that never saw the prompt, three-valued, with each axis blocking on its own -- an averaged styling score would let correct makeup pay for coloured gels. Lighting is where taste meets product truth: flat frontal light erases the shadow that makes crochet texture legible, so it is a product-truth failure wearing a styling costume. The rejected drift examples are read from the audit log because inventing plausible failures would be fabricated evidence and useless besides; which build became canonical is read from the freeze record rather than guessed from which is newest, since a rejected-drift list naming the approved identity as drift would be worse than no list at all. | Yes |
| B-639 | 2026-09-22 | Every registered handler must be runnable by some agent, asserted at commit time. | `creative.photoreal_calibration` was written, registered, boot-enqueued, deployed and dead-lettered in production with "permission denied: agent 'creative_director' may not run it". The guard sweeps the handler registry against the authority matrix and fails on the first orphan. The reverse direction is pinned rather than required: thirteen job types are declared in the matrix and implemented nowhere, which is a deliberate statement of what each agent *would* be permitted rather than a claim the capability exists, so they are listed by name and a fourteenth fails -- a typo or a rename that quietly stopped matching would otherwise surface much later as a permission error on a job somebody believed was wired. | Yes |
| B-640 | 2026-09-22 | A boot job's idempotency key carries the build, so a dead letter cannot outlive the defect that caused it. | `creative.photoreal_calibration` died on B-639's missing permission, and its dead letter kept the key `boot-photoreal-calibration-v1-eleven-named-rejections`. The deploy carrying the fix therefore enqueued nothing and reported `duplicate`: the fix was live and the job it fixed could never run again. The same shape as the version-stamp lesson recorded for the tournament key -- a key that guards the *work* while the work's correctness has changed underneath it -- and the same shape as B-631 one layer up, where a row for a failed attempt stood in for the capability. The cycle-proof and owned-photography keys already carried the commit for exactly this reason; this one did not, and the gap was invisible until the first failure. It remains self-limiting on the real condition rather than on the clock: the boot enqueue asks nothing once a calibration of the current checks exists, so the commit in the key buys a retry after a fix and not a spend on every deploy. | Yes |
| B-641 | 2026-09-22 | A control that cannot answer a check is not a standard that cannot pass, and the photographic-realism judge discriminates. | The calibration's first live run came back `unjudged` on `anatomy_is_possible` and `depth_of_field_is_natural` -- honestly, because the control was a flat-lay swatch with no person and no background in it -- and `calibrate` reported `reachable: false` and "this judge blocked a real photograph". | B-642 | 2026-09-22 | The detail frame is conditioned on the certified chart, and the shot plan stops contradicting the lighting language. | Two findings from the first sequence in which every floor could actually be read. **Product truth failed for a reason that was ours**: the generator produced a handsome three-colour granny-shell fabric for a two-colour certified pattern, having been *told* the motif in a sentence and *graded* against the chart -- asking one question and marking another, which is precisely the gap the identity lock closes by handing over a reference image instead of adjectives. The body needed no deep shadow, not flat light, and those are different requests. | Yes |
| B-643 | 2026-09-22 | Reference-conditioning reproduces a face and does not reproduce a stitch pattern, and that is now #300's weakest link. | The v8 sequence conditioned its detail frame on the certified chart itself -- the deterministic render of the fabric, not a sentence about it -- and the motif judge still called both frames a mismatch. Four of the six floors cleared tonight by finding and fixing a specific defect each; this one did not move, and the difference is what makes it a finding rather than a bad night. It is a capability question about the image provider rather than a prompt to tighten, so it is recorded as the named next decision instead of being chased with the two render attempts still in the budget: the settled provider choice was made on measurement and unpicking it deserves the same, not a hunch at 8am. What the owner's rule protects here is the gate, not the render -- the motif-fidelity check is doing exactly what the checkerboard regression was written for, on a live asset, and nothing about the result is weakened to let a listing through. | Yes |
| B-644 | 2026-09-22 | The motif check grades against the chart, never against a sentence about the object -- and B-643's conclusion was wrong. | An hour after recording that reference-conditioning "reproduces a face and does not reproduce a stitch pattern", the reason turned out to be ours. `expected()` took the chart's motif name from the first sentence of `cir.designer_notes`, and `creative.prototype.author` writes every machine-authored note as "prototype of {key}: {what}, {w} x {h} cm at {gauge}" -- the finished object and its dimensions, with nothing in it about stitches. Two guaranteed failures followed from one value. The render prompt asked for "fabric worked in this pattern's own motif: prototype of hats-hat-0: an adult hat, worked in the round, 52 x 22 cm at 12", so the generator was never told what fabric to make; and the verdict compared an honest description of fabric against those same words, found no overlap and returned `mismatch`. For every product the seasonal cycle authors, `product_truth` could never pass, and it failed for a reason that had nothing to do with the picture -- the fifth floor-nothing-can-clear of the night and the one I had already attributed to the provider. The mirror defect sat behind it: with the name empty the overlap test was skipped and the verdict fell through to MATCH, so an expectation nothing can contradict would have passed every fabric ever rendered. Object prose made it always fail and no prose made it always pass, which are the same defect twice. Now: a note is recognised as object prose by our own format string rather than by hunting for fabric words, because an allowlist of motif vocabulary is a guess about English and the complement of an allowlist is not an allowlist; an unnamed motif reports `name_test: unavailable` rather than passing; and the colour count -- deterministic from the chart, true of every pattern including the ones authored in memory and never filed -- is checked first, which is the fact both live renders actually broke by returning three-colour granny shells for a two-colour hat. B-643 stands only as the question it asked; its answer is withdrawn, and the provider gets asked again after a render that was told the truth. | Yes |
| B-645 | 2026-09-22 | A frame is asked only the styling axes it is in a position to see, and the motif question is now answered rather than inferred. | Two findings from the v9 sequence, the first render told the truth about its own fabric. **The styling fix was incomplete.** Making styling fit-authoritative stopped a close crop's `unjudged` from sinking the floor, and left the crop still being *asked* -- so on the next render it stopped omitting the wardrobe questions and started answering `False`, a photograph of a hat reporting that the outfit is wrong. Under "a failure anywhere blocks everywhere" that is a false block on the whole sequence, and the frame's limitation had arrived dressed as a finding about the styling: the same defect as comparing a body against a portrait, one gate along, and the sixth of the night. `STYLING_AXES` gives each shot the axes it can answer, exactly as `brief.FRAME_AUTHORITY` already does for the identity dimensions; an axis not asked reports `not_asked`, which is neither a pass nor an unjudged. Narrowing what is asked is not narrowing what counts -- makeup, expression and lighting are visible in a crop that includes her face, and a breach in any of them still blocks. **And the motif verdict is now real.** With the prompt pointing at the chart and the colour count stated, both frames came back `same_pattern_as_chart: false` from the judge directly rather than from a zero-overlap artefact -- a granny-square flower motif on one and a diamond cross on the other, neither the certified chart. So B-643's question is properly open again on evidence that means what it says: told the truth, conditioned on the chart image, and given the colour count, this provider still renders a different fabric. That is the next decision and it now rests on a measurement rather than on a defect. | Yes |
| B-646 | 2026-09-22 | Every frame in the sequence is conditioned on one chart, rendered once -- and the provider question is closed. | The v10 detail frame returned `product_truth: pass`: the judge compared the photograph against the chart itself, said the fabric is working it, and the colour count agreed. That floor had never cleared before, and it closes a question I had got wrong twice -- once blaming the provider for a defect of mine (B-643), then reinstating the suspicion on the v9 evidence (B-645). Both times the answer was that the render had not been told what fabric to make. What the same sequence exposed is that the fit frame still had not been: I withheld the chart from it on the reasoning that a chart among a portrait's references would pull the frame towards a flat swatch and that product truth is not that frame's floor. The second half was wrong and the run said so -- the fit frame's fabric is read too and its `mismatch` blocks the sequence, because both frames ship. Not being a floor's authority is not the same as not being judged by it, which is the inverse of the mistake `_combine_floors` was written to avoid and was sitting in the conditioning rather than in the verdict. Worse than the fidelity failure is the coherence one underneath it: the two frames are independent renders, so one conditioned on the chart and one not produced two different fabrics inside a single listing. Both frames now see the chart, and it is rendered once for the sequence rather than once per frame -- two renders of one chart would almost certainly agree, and "almost certainly" is not what a listing claiming to show one product needs from the reference both of its pictures are built on. | Yes |
| B-647 | 2026-09-22 | A judged benchmark image is marked judged, and the remaining count is counted rather than computed to fall. | Found by reading the cadence outputs after eight unattended hours rather than by trusting a figure I had already repeated. `gallery_analysed` was read in `pending()` and written nowhere, so every two-hourly run took the same twenty-five images off the same newest listings, judged them, paid for them and left them pending -- four paid batches moved the backlog from 475 to 476, about CA$8.50 a day indefinitely for no new evidence. It was invisible from outside because `judged: 25` is exactly what a draining queue reports, and the second half of the defect completed the illusion: `remaining` read the real backlog and subtracted this run's own `judged` from it, so the number fell by twenty-five every run whether or not anything had been recorded. A progress figure computed to look like progress is worse than no figure, and this one reached the owner as "about 38 hours and CA$13 to empty" -- a number I passed on from a single sample without checking that the second had moved. Marking is per image rank rather than per listing, because a batch limit lands mid-gallery: a listing-level flag would re-offer a whole gallery whose last image failed and abandon the remainder of one the limit cut short. A listing is analysed only when every image it offers has been judged, counted rather than inferred from the last one. | Yes |
| B-648 | 2026-09-23 | The health sweep distinguishes completed work from produced evidence, and a loop repeating itself reads as idle rather than healthy. | The forensic question was why a day of visible activity produced little, and every failure candidate was ruled out by production evidence: deployed SHA matched local and remote, the worker had nine hours uptime and zero restarts, the scheduler ticked, the queue held nothing, `/api/verify` was 12/12, and `build_tick` correctly reported five READY requirements. Nothing was broken. What happened is that forty-four cadences ran and the system learned almost nothing: `gallery_analysis` re-judged the same twenty-five images twelve times and charged each time (B-647), five cadences were no-ops the queue already names in `last_run_was_a_no_op`, and the seasonal proof failed at the same link as before. Through all of it `ops.health` reported `state: healthy` with the reason "work is being completed" -- because `progress` counts completed jobs, and completed jobs are not produced evidence. That gap is the root cause of the perception, and it is the same defect family as the rest of this log: a verdict computed from a proxy that cannot see the thing it stands for. `evidence_freshness` compares each cadence's latest result against its previous one across a twelve-hour window, so a cadence returning what it returned last time is visible as producing nothing. It is deliberately not a judgement about value -- "different from last time" is checkable without opinion, and the failure it exists to catch is exactly a result that never moves. The window is twelve hours rather than six because several cadences are daily, so six hours of identical daily results is normal and twelve is not. | Yes |
| B-649 | 2026-09-23 | The market map's vision coverage is counted from derived attributes, not asserted absent. | Found while production-proving B-647's drain, by asking why `silhouette` and `merchandising_mechanism` still read "absent on all 438" after days of paid gallery analysis. They were not measured: `gaps()` emitted `absent_on: len(observed), share: 1.0` for both columns as a literal, so the number could not move however many galleries were judged. At the moment I read it, one listing in the table carried twenty recorded observations from a single run and the report still said the attribute was absent everywhere. That is paid evidence existing while the one report that says whether the spend buys anything insists we know nothing -- stale state presented as current, and the direct reason nobody could tell whether the drain was working. It named the wrong blocker as well, `capability: browser/vision`, when the vision capability had been demonstrated and `intel.gallery_analysis` was already running on it; a closeable gap was pointing at a gate that had opened. Now derived per listing through `vision.attributes_for`, which respects the existing two-image floor rather than counting a single judged frame as knowledge. The test that asserted the stale wording was corrected rather than deleted: it had pinned `capability == "browser/vision"` as its contrast case, so the suite would have kept the expired claim alive -- the third time a test has preserved words that stopped being true (B-606, B-620). | Yes |
| B-650 | 2026-09-23 | A frame that cleared every floor it answers is kept rather than rendered again, and a frame with any failed floor is never kept. | The architectural answer to render oscillation, and the owner's framing is what makes it the right one: a pipeline does not need a perfect first-attempt rate if bad generations are reliably rejected and regeneration is economically acceptable; what is unacceptable is a bad generation reaching a customer through a gate that wrongly passed it. Generation is stochastic and the six floors are independent, so re-rendering the whole sequence on any failure asks all six to land in a single draw -- which is exactly what the live runs showed: v8 cleared identity, morphology, asset truth and styling; v10 cleared product truth; no one draw cleared everything. Keeping what passed converts "all six floors in one attempt" into "each frame passes in some attempt", which is the same standard at a fraction of the cost, and it is the difference between a lucky render and a pipeline. Nothing is lowered: a kept frame keeps its own verdicts and they are re-combined on every pass, `unverifiable` is still not a pass, and reuse is safe for gallery continuity because a kept frame was conditioned on the same frozen pack, body reference and certified chart as the frame rendered beside it. The subtle half is the disqualification rule. A frame is rejected for *any* failed floor, including one it is not the authority for: a fit frame whose fabric read as a mismatch is a bad photograph, and keeping it would carry that `fail` into every later sequence under the "a failure anywhere blocks" rule -- poisoning the release permanently with a verdict nothing could ever clear. Caught by the first version of the test rather than in production, which is the first time this session a defect of that shape was stopped before it shipped. | Yes |
| B-651 | 2026-09-23 | The render-reliability standard is fixed in code, with its thresholds and their reasoning, before any measurement is taken. | A rate measured without a standard becomes its own standard, which is how every disappointing number gets explained rather than acted on. Two standards, kept apart on purpose: CORRECTNESS (no asset ever marked usable with a floor that did not say `pass`) is absolute and one breach blocks launch however good the rate, because that is the failure that reaches a buyer; ECONOMICS (a usable gallery inside 3 attempts at no more than CA$2.00) is reasoned from a ~CA$9.50 pattern whose imagery is made once and serves the listing for its whole life. The owner's 2026-09-23 framing is what makes this tractable — a pipeline does not need a perfect first-attempt rate if bad generations are reliably rejected and regeneration is affordable. | Thresholds yes, on evidence; the correctness standard no |
| B-652 | 2026-09-23 | A dimension's failures are counted against the attempts that asked it, never against all attempts; and a dimension asked fewer than twice is reported unclassified rather than classified. | The first draft of B-651 carried the defect B-651 exists to catch. `product_truth` is decided by the detail frame, so a floor asked three times and failed three times would have read as a 27% stochastic failure in an eleven-attempt sample — an architecture problem diagnosed as bad luck and retried forever. The wrong-denominator family again, after `remaining` subtracting its own run's work and a coverage share reading 1.0 because nothing had been observed. The second half is the same refusal from the other side: one ask cannot tell a systematic failure from a stochastic one, and guessing either way sends the next session to rebuild an architecture on a sample of one. | No |
| B-653 | 2026-09-23 | Frame reuse reads the frames nested inside the filed sequence record, and its test drives the real filing path. | B-650 shipped a reader that expected one audit row per frame. Green test, dead feature, because the test filed frames one at a time: a shape production never writes. Fixed on the read side rather than the write side, which also makes the sequences already filed in production reusable instead of needing them re-rendered. | B-654 | 2026-09-23 | A floor that has failed every time the current method was asked stops the method rendering at all, across every release, until `METHOD_VERSION` changes. | `ATTEMPTS = 3` bounds one release and does nothing about a method that does not work: each new product starts its budget again, so the next product pays three more times for the same answer. Live evidence: every model frame this system has produced, across v8 to v12, blocked on `photographic_realism` against direction that names airbrushed skin at paragraph length, and a judge the calibration proved can pass a real photograph. (Only one of those is a v12 sequence, so this block is not in force today — the measurement counts only the current method, by design, and B-658's reference verdict is what actually stops the spend.) That is B-651's `systematic` classification, and its prescribed response is a code change rather than a retry. The escape is deliberately work rather than waiting — the measurement counts only the current method, so cutting a new `METHOD_VERSION` is what clears it, and that is somebody changing the method. | Yes, by changing the method |
| B-655 | 2026-09-23 | `METHOD_VERSION` is not bumped for a fix that addresses unjudged checks while the failed ones stand. | Two shot plans were corrected in the same commit — neither asked for the hands that `asset_truth` and `photographic_realism` both ask about, so both floors could only ever be cleared by the generator including something nobody requested. But hands were coming back *unjudged*; the systematic block is on checks coming back *failed*. Bumping the version would have cleared B-654's block without fixing what it caught and bought three more attempts with unauthorised money — the author defeating his own gate on the day it was built. The direction is staged for the version cut when the realism cause is actually addressed. | Yes |
| B-656 | 2026-09-23 | The frozen identity pack is judged by the same photographic-realism standard its renders are held to, and only the subject's own properties are treated as inheritable. | Three renders failed `skin_looks_real` against direction naming the defect explicitly, which admits two explanations needing opposite fixes: the provider will not do unretouched skin, or the reference it is conditioned on is already airbrushed. The inheritable set is narrow on purpose: lighting, depth of field, invented text and sterile perfection belong to the scene the new frame builds around her, and counting those would let a real generator failure hide behind an irrelevant flaw in the reference, which is a verdict drawn from evidence about something else. | No |
| B-657 | 2026-09-23 | A reference pack whose own images fail the realism checks a render inherits from them is never frozen as canonical. | Proven in production, not argued: `/api/reference-realism` read pack v15 and both frames came back `blocked` on `skin_looks_real` and `processing_is_restrained`, the body frame also on `hands_are_right` — the exact three that had blocked every model frame. The twin recorded `position` in working order and every consumer read it as if it were fabric order. Found by the benchmark cardigan, whose hem ribbing is integral -- nine back-loop stitches at one edge of every row. In working order those nine landed at positions 0-8 on odd rows and 80-88 on even ones, so the twin described a rib zig-zagging from edge to edge instead of the continuous band the garment visibly has. The measured consequence was not subtle: the fabric's texture signature read `offset_between_rows: 0.085` -- stripes -- and reading the same cells in fabric coordinates gave 0.864, a checkered surface, which is both what the benchmark's stitch-pattern name describes and what the photographs show. The same error mirrors alternate rows of `color_grid`, which feeds chart rendering, so this is shipped functionality that has been drawing flat two-colour charts with every other row reversed, not merely a missing feature. Rounds are deliberately exempt: they do not turn, so working order already is fabric order and mirroring them would invent a reversal the fabric does not have. | No |
| B-691 | 2026-09-24 | Loop targeting (BLO/FLO) becomes a count-neutral field on the op, and the crochet region of a product image is drawn from the compiled twin rather than generated. | Three providers and twelve renders returned product truth 0 of 12 (B-689), and the diagnosis was that asking a diffusion model to draw a specific combinatorial stitch structure is a model guessing the instructions -- forbidden for pattern text by the Execution Directive, and never made permissible for pattern imagery. The benchmark cardigan then showed the gap precisely: its entire visual identity is alternating BLO/FLO half doubles, and the CIR could represent every stitch count in the garment exactly while having no way to say which loop any stitch entered. Count-perfect and texture-blind is the worst combination available, because it passes every arithmetic gate and renders the wrong fabric. The field is deliberately count-neutral -- front, back and both all consume one and produce one -- so the compiler's arithmetic is untouched and no existing certified pattern needs re-verification to carry texture; a decorative property that changed a stitch count would make the one guarantee this system offers depend on a cosmetic field. `visual/fabric.py` then draws one rect per counted stitch and one bar per unworked loop, with cell size computed from the same gauge arithmetic as the finished-size claim so the swatch and the measurement cannot disagree. It is flat, unlit and unlovely on purpose: it answers whether structure can be authoritative, not whether it can be beautiful. Presentation may later composite, light and stage around this region, but may never redraw it, because a presentation layer that redraws the fabric is the guessing path with a new name. | No |
| B-692 | 2026-09-24 | A texture is classified from both fabric axes, and no claim is made about direction on the worn garment. | The first version thresholded on alternation along a row alone and reported `ridges_run: up_the_rows` for a fabric whose loop target also flipped on every following row -- which is a checkerboard with no persisting column, not a ridge. A single threshold cannot distinguish them, so the verdict was asserting a direction the measurement could not support: the same defect as a verdict computed from evidence nobody gathered, in a new place. It now reads alternation and between-row offset together and names the surface from both. It also refuses to say whether the texture runs vertically on the finished garment, because that depends on the panel's grain direction and the CIR does not carry one -- and the benchmark is exactly the case where guessing would be wrong in an interesting way, since a side-to-side body makes its rows run up the garment rather than around it. Naming the missing primitive is worth more than a plausible answer. | No |
| B-693 | 2026-09-24 | Recorded correction: I over-restricted the benchmark and weakened the test. The full purchased pattern is encoded; only the seller's written text and photographs stay out. | I treated the whole purchased pattern as protected expression and reduced it to high-level measurements, which was wrong twice over. Legally it conflates facts with authorship: stitch counts, gauge, construction method, stitch sequence, grading and assembly are facts about how a garment is made, and the protected thing is the seller's particular written text and their photographs. Practically it threw away the benchmark's whole value — the owner bought the pattern precisely so the system could study it, and a summary cannot test a reconstruction. The full encoding immediately paid for itself by surfacing three things a summary had already lost: the cuff is slip stitch rather than half double, which is the entire mechanism of the balloon sleeve and would otherwise have produced a correct stitch count and a sleeve hanging like a pipe; the hem ribbing is worked into every body row rather than added as a band; and the neckband's constant back-loop working is ribbing, which the texture classifier was calling flat. The rule that remains is the one that was always the real one: do not republish their text or pass their photographs off as ours, and any Brambleloop product informed by this gets its own design decisions, its own instructions and its own validation rather than a paraphrase. | Yes, on evidence |
| B-694 | 2026-09-24 | Panel grain is a first-class CIR property, and a chain span is still not. | Grain — whether a panel's rows stack up the body or around it — costs nothing in stitch counts and decides the single most visible property of a textured garment in a photograph. This body is worked side to side, so its rows run vertically on the worn garment and every row boundary is a vertical line; a bottom-up panel with identical counts would look completely different. Without the field the CIR could describe this cardigan perfectly and not know which way its fabric ran, so nothing downstream could honestly claim a direction. The chain span is the gap that remains and it is now measured rather than predicted: the armhole is bridged by chains that replace the stitches the row skipped, the CIR counts each chain as one unit of width, and the twin therefore overstates the body by +1.11% at XS and +1.02% at 5XL. The pattern's own numbers imply a chain gauge of 17.6-19.7 per 10cm against 14.5 for worked fabric. Named and left unbuilt: the experiment was authorised as a bounded proof, and a primitive that changes how every existing pattern measures is not a thing to add in the margins of one. | No |
| B-695 | 2026-09-24 | The chart-order defect is confirmed customer-facing by the charts' own footer, not by reasoning about convention. | B-690 asserted that drawing flat charts in working order was a live defect. That was a claim about customer output made from reasoning, and this codebase's rule is to test the flattering-to-me answer rather than act on it. The evidence turned out to be sitting in the renderer: `charts.py` prints "odd rows read right to left, even rows read left to right" on every colour chart it produces, and that footer is an explicit promise about direction which only holds if the grid says where stitches ARE rather than the order they were made in. So the chart was instructing makers to read even rows one way while drawing them the other, in the PDF pattern, the listing assets and the motif-fidelity check alike. A convention argument would have been suggestive; a contradiction between two artefacts the system itself emits is proof. `test_twin_chart_grid_shape_matches_row_counts` had encoded the working-order behaviour and was updated -- a test asserting the defect is how a defect survives, and it is the fourth time this session a passing test has been found pinning the wrong behaviour. | Yes, on evidence |
